\vec{F}=\frac{\vec{e}_{r}}{r^{\lambda}}

are rotation free in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{O\}
. This is particularly interesting for e.g.
Modified Newtonian Dynamics
, or MOND for short, which aims, as an alternative to the so–called
dark matter
model, at explaining the high velocities of the stars at the outskirts of galaxies. In this MOND model it is proposed to have, from a certain treshold on in the acceleration, the gravitational field to be proportional to

\frac{\vec{e}_{r}}{r}

As has been showed here, it is clear that whatever power of the radial distance is used in a gravitational field, it always will obey the zero energy orbiting principle.

12.2

In three dimensional space the formulae for the second order cartesian derivatives of the fundamental solution of the Laplace operator
T_{-m+2}
, obtained in subsection 9.3, viz.

\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{j}}\,T_{-m+2}=m(m-2)\,x_{k}\,x_{j}\,T_{-m-2}\quad,\quad j\neq k

and

\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}\,T_{-m+2}=m(m-2)\,x_{j}^{2}\ T_{-m-2}-(m-2)\,T_{-m}-\frac{m-2}{m}\,a_{m}\ \delta(\underline{x})

turn into

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{j}}\,T_{-1}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{k}\,x_{j}\,T_{-5}\quad,\quad j\neq k

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}\,T_{-1}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{j}^{2}\,T_{-5}-T_{-3}-\frac{4}{3}\,\pi\ \delta(\underline{x})

or, in the new notation,

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{j}}\,\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{k}\,x_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{5}}\quad,\quad j\neq k

(115)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}\,\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{j}^{2}\,\frac{1}{r^{5}}-\frac{4}{3}\,\pi\,\delta(\underline{x})-\frac{1}{r^{3}}

(116)

These expressions for the second order partial derivatives of the Coulomb potential of a unit point charge are of course well–known in physics, see e.g.
[
17
,
19
,
1
]
.
In
[
18
]
it is argued that, with respect to test functions that are not smooth at the origin, the above formulae should be replaced by

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{j}}\,\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{k}\,x_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{5}}-4\,\pi\,\frac{x_{j}\,x_{k}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})\quad,\quad j\neq k

(117)

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}\,\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle=3\,x_{j}^{2}\,\frac{1}{r^{5}}-4\,\pi\,\frac{x_{j}^{2}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})-\frac{1}{r^{3}}

(118)

Mathematically speaking it is not clear what in
[
18
]
is meant by ”test functions that are not smooth at the origin“. Nevertheless, taking into account that the delta distribution and its derivatives are only defined on differentiable test functions, it is readily observed that
formulae (
117
) and (
118
) simply
coincide
with formulae (
115
) and (
116
), seen the following results.

Lemma 3
.

For the delta distribution
\delta(\underline{x})
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
one has

(i)
x_{j}^{2}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=2\,\delta(\underline{x})

(ii)
x_{j}\,x_{k}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=0\quad,\quad j\neq k

(iii)
r^{2}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=2\,m\,\delta(\underline{x})

(iv)
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{2m}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})

(v)
\frac{x_{j}^{2}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

(vi)
\frac{x_{j}\,x_{k}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=0\quad,\quad j\neq k

Proof

(i)
For any test function
\varphi(\underline{x})
and
j=1,\ldots,m
it holds that

\langle\ x_{j}^{2}\,\Delta\delta\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle=\langle\ \delta\ ,\ \Delta\,(x_{j}^{2}\,\varphi)\ \rangle=\langle\ \delta\ ,\ 2\,\varphi+4\,x_{j}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi+x_{j}^{2}\,\Delta\varphi\ \rangle=2\,\langle\ \delta\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle

(ii)
One has

\langle\ x_{j}\,x_{k}\,\Delta\delta\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle=\langle\ \delta\ ,\ \Delta\,(x_{j}\,x_{k}\,\,\varphi)\ \rangle=\langle\ \delta\ ,2\,x_{k}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi+2\,x_{j}\,\partial_{x_{k}}\varphi+x_{j}\,x_{k}\,\,\Delta\varphi\ \rangle=0

(iii)
It follows from
(i)
that

r^{2}\,\Delta\,\delta=\Sigma_{j=1}^{m}\,x_{j}^{2}\,\Delta\,\delta=2\,m\,\delta

(iv)
Taking into account the homogeinity of the delta distribution, it follows from
(iii)
that

\Delta\,\delta=2\,m\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta

(v)
It follows from
(iv)
and
(i)
that

\frac{x_{j}^{2}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=x_{j}^{2}\,\frac{1}{2m}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

(vi)
Follows from
(iv)
and
(ii)
.

\square

As a corollary it then follows from
(vi)
that the extra term in (
117
) compared to (
115
) is indeed zero. Putting
m=3
in
(v)
shows that

4\,\pi\,\frac{x_{j}^{2}}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{4}{3}\,\pi\,\delta(\underline{x})

thus making (
118
) coincide with (
116
).

12.3

An interesting formula in
[
18
]
is

\vec{\nabla}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}=4\,\pi\,\frac{1}{r}\,\vec{e}_{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})-3\,\vec{e}_{r}\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}

(119)

How does it fit into the present theory? Start with formula (
11
), viz.

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-m}=-\,m\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\,\delta(\underline{x})

Put
m=3
and make use of

\frac{1}{m}\,\underline{\partial}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{\underline{x}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=-\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})

to obtain

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-3}=-\,3\,U_{-4}+4\,\pi\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})

which in the vector notation reads

\vec{\nabla}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}=-3\,\vec{e}_{r}\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}+4\,\pi\,\frac{1}{r}\,\vec{e}_{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})

which is exactly (
119
). In
[
18
]
this result is also written as

\vec{\nabla}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}=g(r)\,\vec{e}_{r}

with

g(r)=\vec{e}_{r}\circ\vec{\nabla}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}=-3\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}+4\,\pi\,\frac{1}{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})

Clearly
g(r)
is the radial signumdistribution

-3\,^{s}T_{-4}+4\,\pi\,\frac{1}{r}\,\delta(\underline{x})=-\,\underline{\omega}\,(\underline{\partial}\,T_{-3})

12.4

The electric potential induced by the electric dipole moment
\vec{p}
is given by

\phi(\vec{x})=\vec{p}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{3}}

and the corresponding electric field is

\vec{E}=-\,\vec{\nabla}\,\phi

In
[
17
]
it is shown that this electric field may be expressed directly in terms of the electric dipole moment as

\vec{E}=-\,\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{p}+3\left(\vec{p}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{5}}\right)\vec{x}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,\vec{p}

It is our aim now to recover this expression through the formulae obtained in the underlying paper. Let
\underline{p}
be the constant Clifford vector corresponding with the vector
\vec{p}
. Then, in general dimension
m
, it holds that

\phi=\vec{p}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{m}}=-\,\underline{p}\cdot U_{-m+1}

From

\partial_{x_{i}}\,(\underline{p}\,U_{-m+1})=\underline{p}\,\partial_{x_{i}}U_{-m+1}=\underline{p}\,(T_{-m}\,e_{i}-m\,x_{i}\,U_{-m-1}+\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,e_{i})

it follows that

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\phi=-\,\underline{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{i}}\,U_{-m+1}=p_{i}\,T_{-m}+m\,x_{i}\,(\underline{p}\cdot U_{-m-1})+\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,p_{i}\,\delta(\underline{x})

whence

\underline{E}=-\,\underline{p}\,T_{-m}-m\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x})\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\underline{p}

since

(\underline{p}\cdot U_{-m-1})\,\underline{x}=(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x})\,U_{-m-1}

For
m=3
and in vector notation we indeed obtain

\vec{E}=-\,\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{p}+3\left(\vec{p}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{5}}\right)\vec{x}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,\vec{p}

In
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{O\}
the electric field
\vec{E}
is divergence and rotation free, since it is the gradient field of a harmonic scalar field
\phi
. So if we compute the divergence and the rotation of
\vec{E}
as a distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we expect the results to be distributions supported at the origin. We can compute both at the same time by acting with the Dirac operator on
\underline{E}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\underline{\partial}\,\underline{E}

\displaystyle=-\,\left(-\,m\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\right)\,\underline{p}+m\,\underline{p}\,U_{-m-1}-\,m\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x})\,\left(2\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{1}{2m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\delta(\underline{x})\right)-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\,\underline{p}

\displaystyle=2\,m\,\underline{p}\cdot U_{-m-1}-2\,m\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x})\,T_{-m-2}-\,\frac{1}{2}\,a_{m}\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x})\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,a_{m}\,\Sigma\,p_{j}\,x_{j}\,\Delta\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=-\,a_{m}\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{\partial})\delta(\underline{x})

from which it follows that

\underline{\partial}\cdot\underline{E}=-\,a_{m}\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{\partial})\delta(\underline{x})\quad{\rm and}\quad\underline{\partial}\wedge\underline{E}=0

or in dimension 3 and in vector analysis language:

\vec{\nabla}\circ\vec{E}=-\,4\pi(\vec{p}\circ\vec{\nabla})\delta(\underline{x})\quad{\rm and}\quad\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{E}=0

showing that the electric field of an electric dipole is rotation free in the whole space even if it is not differentiable at the origin. It’s divergence may also be obtained via

\vec{\nabla}\circ\vec{E}=-\,\Delta\,\phi=\underline{p}\cdot\Delta\,U_{-m+1}=a_{m}\,(\underline{p}\cdot\underline{\partial})\delta(\underline{x})=-a_{m}\,(\vec{p}\circ\vec{\nabla})\delta(\underline{x})

In a similar way if
\vec{M}
stands for the magnetic dipole moment, the magnetic vector potential is given by

\vec{A}=\vec{M}\,\times\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{3}}

and the corresponding magnetic field is

\vec{B}=\vec{\nabla}\,\times\,\vec{A}

We will now recover the formula, appearing in
[
17
]
, expressing the magnetic field in terms of the magnetic dipole moment:

\vec{B}=\frac{8\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{M}+3\,(\vec{M}\circ\vec{x})\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{5}}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,\vec{M}

First we observe that

\vec{B}=\vec{\nabla}\,\times\,\vec{A}=\vec{\nabla}\,\times\,(\vec{M}\,\times\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{3}})=\left(\vec{\nabla}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{3}}\right)\,\vec{M}-\left(\vec{M}\circ\vec{\nabla}\right)\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{3}}

In general dimension
m
we have

\vec{\nabla}\circ\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{m}}=-\,\left[\underline{\partial}\,U_{-m+1}\right]_{0}=a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

and

\displaystyle\left(\vec{M}\circ\vec{\nabla}\right)\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{m}}

\displaystyle=\left(\Sigma_{j=1}^{m}\,M_{j}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\right)\,U_{-m+1}=\Sigma_{j=1}^{m}\,M_{j}\,\left(T_{-m}\,e_{j}-m\,x_{j}\,U_{-m-1}+\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,e_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{-m}\,\vec{M}-m\,\left(\Sigma_{j=1}^{m}\,M_{j}\,x_{j}\right)\,U_{-m-1}+\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{M}

whence

\underline{B}=\frac{m-1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\underline{M}-T_{-m}\,\underline{M}+m\,(\vec{M}\circ\vec{x})\,U_{-m-1}

and so, putting
m=3
,

\displaystyle\vec{B}

\displaystyle=4\pi\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{M}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,\vec{M}+3\,(\vec{M}\circ\vec{x})\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{5}}-\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})\,\vec{M}+3\,(\vec{M}\circ\vec{x})\,\frac{\vec{x}}{r^{5}}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,\vec{M}

This magnetic field
\vec{B}
satisfies the Maxwell equation

\vec{\nabla}\circ\vec{B}=0

which would be trivial since
\vec{B}
is a rotation field, were it not that
\vec{B}
is not differentiable at the origin. To show that the magnetic field is indeed divergence-free we act with the Dirac operator on
\underline{B}
and obtain

\displaystyle\underline{\partial}\,\underline{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{m-1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\underline{M}-\left(-\,m\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\right)\,\underline{M}+m\,\Sigma\,e_{j}\,M_{j}\,U_{-m-1}-m\,(\underline{M}\cdot\underline{x})\left(2\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{m+2}{2m(m+2)}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\delta(\underline{x})\right)

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\,\underline{M}+m\,\left(U_{-m-1}\,\underline{M}+\underline{M}\,U_{-m-1}\right)-2\,m\,(\underline{M}\cdot\underline{x})\,T_{-m-2}+a_{m}\,\Sigma\,M_{j}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\,\underline{M}-a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\cdot\underline{M}

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\wedge\underline{M}

from which it follows that

\underline{\partial}\cdot\underline{B}=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\underline{\partial}\wedge\underline{B}=a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\wedge\underline{M}

or, in the vector analysis language of three dimensional space,

\vec{\nabla}\circ\vec{B}=0\quad{\rm and}\quad\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}=4\pi\,\vec{\nabla}\delta(\underline{x})\times\vec{M}=-\,4\pi\,(\vec{M}\times\vec{\nabla})\delta(\underline{x})

12.5

In
[
1
]
a number of identities for the delta distribution are given as corollaries to the computation of integrals of spherical Bessel functions. It is shown e.g. that

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}-\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{ij}\,\Delta\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}=\frac{8}{r^{4}}\,\left(\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,-\frac{1}{3}\ \delta_{ij}\right)

or, taking into account that in general
\Delta\,T_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,T_{-m-1}
, and thus in three dimensional space:
\Delta\,T_{-2}=2\,T_{-4}
,

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}=\frac{8}{r^{4}}\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,-2\,\delta_{ij}\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}

We verify these formulae as follows. If
i\neq j
then for the distribution
T_{-m+1}
it holds that

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,T_{-m+1}=(-m+1)(-m-1)\,x_{i}\,x_{j}\,T_{-m-3}

which for
m=3
turns into

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,T_{-2}=8\,x_{i}\,x_{j}\,T_{-6}=8\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,T_{-4}

If
i=j
then we have

\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}\,T_{-m+1}=(-m+1)(-m-1)\,x_{i}^{2}\,T_{-m-3}+(-m+1)\,T_{-m-1}

which for
m=3
turns into

\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}\,T_{-2}=8\,x_{i}^{2}\,T_{-6}-2\,T_{-4}=8\,\omega_{i}^{2}\,T_{-4}-2\,T_{-4}

It is also shown in
[
1
]
that

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\delta(\underline{x})-\frac{1}{3}\,\delta_{ij}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=15\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\delta(\underline{x})-5\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(\underline{x})

or, taking into account that in general
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{2m}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})
, and thus in three dimensional space:
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{6}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})
,

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\delta(\underline{x})+\frac{1}{2}\,\delta_{ij}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})=15\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\delta(\underline{x})

We verify these formulae as follows. If
i\neq j
then

\displaystyle\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=x_{i}\,x_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=x_{i}\,x_{j}\,\frac{1}{8m(m+2)}\,\underline{\partial}^{4}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{m(m+2)}\,x_{i}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{m(m+2)}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\delta(\underline{x})

which for
m=3
reduces to

\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{15}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{j}}\,\delta(\underline{x})

For
i=j
we find

\displaystyle\omega_{i}\,\omega_{i}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=x_{i}\,x_{i}\,\frac{1}{r^{4}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=x_{i}\,x_{i}\,\frac{1}{8m(m+2)}\,\underline{\partial}^{4}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{m(m+2)}\,x_{i}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{2m(m+2)}\,\left(2\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\delta(\underline{x})+\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m(m+2)}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})+\frac{1}{m(m+2)}\,\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})

which for
m=3
reduces to

\omega_{i}^{2}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=\frac{1}{30}\,\Delta\,\delta(\underline{x})+\frac{1}{15}\,\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})

12.6

In
[
2
]
a formula is proved for

\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(\omega_{j_{1}}\cdots\omega_{j_{n}}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)

the inverse square field
\frac{1}{r^{2}}
being at the heart of the discussion of idealized point sources in three dimensional space. An elegant proof based on more general formulae is given in
[
16
]
. We show how this formula fits into our theory focussing on two special cases:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(\omega_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}-3\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}+\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\displaystyle\ \partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,\omega_{k}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}+\delta_{ik}\,\omega_{j}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}-4\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,\frac{1}{r^{3}}

or, in the language of the underlying paper:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,T_{-3}-3\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,T_{-3}+\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(\underline{x})

(120)

\displaystyle\ \partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,T_{-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}+\delta_{ik}\,\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-3}-4\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}

(121)

It is important to remark that in (
121
) the expression
\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,T_{-2}
is a distribution. Indeed, it holds for the distribution
T_{-2}
that

\omega_{k}\,T_{-2}=\{\underline{\omega}\,T_{-2}\}^{(k)}=\{^{s}U_{-2}\}^{(k)}=x_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}

whence

\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,T_{-2}=\{\underline{\omega}\,x_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}\}^{(j)}=\{x_{k}\,U_{-3}\}^{(j)}=x_{j}\,x_{k}\,T_{-3}

However, if in (
120
)
T_{-2}
would be interpreted as a distribution, then the expression
\omega_{j}\,T_{-2}
becomes a signumdistribution. In order to obtain the proposed result (
120
),
\omega_{j}\,T_{-2}
should be a distribution whence
\frac{1}{r^{2}}
has to be interpreted as the signumdistribution
{}^{s}T_{-2}
. It then holds indeed that

\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-2}=\{\underline{\omega}\,^{s}T_{-2}\}^{(j)}=\{U_{-2}\}^{(j)}=x_{j}\,T_{-3}

clearly a distribution.

To prove (
120
) consider the signumdistribution
{}^{s}T_{-m+1}
for which it holds that

\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-m+1}=x_{j}\,T_{-m}

For
i\neq j
we then have

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{j}\ ^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(x_{j}\ T_{-m}\right)=x_{j}\,\left(-\,m\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-2}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\delta(\underline{x})\right)=-\,m\,x_{i}\,x_{j}\,T_{-m-2}

since
x_{j}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\delta(\underline{x})=0
. It follows that

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=-\,m\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,T_{-m}

which for
m=3
turns into

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{j}\,^{s}T_{-2}\right)=-\,3\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,T_{-3}

For
i=j
we have

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{i}\ ^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(x_{i}\ T_{-m}\right)=T_{-m}+x_{i}\,\left(-\,m\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-2}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\delta(\underline{x})\right)=T_{-m}-\,m\,x_{i}^{2}\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

since
x_{i}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\,\delta(\underline{x})=-\,\delta(\underline{x})
. Putting
m=3
we obtain

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{i}\ ^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=T_{-3}-3\,\omega_{i}^{2}\,T_{-3}+\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\delta(\underline{x})

Formula (
121
) is proven in a similar way. If
i\neq j
and
i\neq k
then

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{k}\,\omega_{j}\ ^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(x_{k}\,x_{j}\ T_{-m-1}\right)=x_{k}\,x_{j}\,(-m-1)\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-3}=-\,(m+1)\,x_{i}\,x_{j}\,x_{k}\,T_{-m-3}=-\,(m+1)\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-m}

since it easily seen that
\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-m}=x_{i}\,x_{j}\,x_{k}\,T_{-m-3}
.
For
m=3
we obtain

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{k}\,\omega_{j}\ ^{s}T_{-2}\right)=-\,4\,\omega_{i}\,\omega_{j}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}

If
i=j
and
i\neq k
then

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{k}\,\omega_{i}\ ^{s}T_{-m+1}\right)=\partial_{x_{i}}\,\left(x_{k}\,x_{i}\ T_{-m-1}\right)=x_{k}\,T_{-m-1}+x_{k}\,x_{i}\,(-m-1)\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-3}=\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-m}-\,(m+1)\,\omega_{i}^{2}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-m}

which for
m=3
reduces to

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{k}\,\omega_{i}\ ^{s}T_{-2}\right)=\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}-\,4\,\omega_{i}^{2}\,\omega_{k}\,^{s}T_{-3}

Finaly if
i=j=k
then

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{i}^{2}\,T_{-m+1}\right)=\partial_{x_{i}}\left(x_{i}^{2}\,T_{-m-1}\right)=2\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-1}+x_{i}^{2}\,(-m-1)\,x_{i}\,T_{-m-3}=2\,\omega_{i}\,^{s}T_{-m}-(m+1)\,\omega_{i}^{3}\,T_{-m}

which for
m=3
turns into

\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\omega_{i}^{2}\,T_{-2}\right)=2\,\omega_{i}\,^{s}T_{-3}-4\,\omega_{i}^{3}\,T_{-3}

These considerations show that formulae such as (
120
) and (
121
) should be handled with great care, especially with regard to the distributional or signumdistributional character of negative powers of the radial distance
r
. In this respect it holds more generally that

\omega_{j_{1}}\cdots\omega_{j_{2n}}\,T_{\lambda}=x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{2n}}\,T_{\lambda-2n}

is a distribution, while

\omega_{j_{1}}\cdots\omega_{j_{2n+1}}\,T_{\lambda}=x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{2n+1}}\,^{s}T_{\lambda-2n-1}

is a signumdistribution.

13
Conclusion

Expressing distributions in Euclidean space in terms of spherical co-ordinates inevitably leads to the introduction of so–called signumdistributions, i.e. bounded linear functionals on a space of indefinitely differentiable test functions showing a non–removable singularity at the origin. Operators acting on distributions appear in two kinds. The cartesian operators can be expressed using cartesian co-ordinates solely. They map distributions to distributions and their action may be either uniquely determined, as is the case for e.g. derivation with respect to the cartesian co-ordinates, multiplication by analytic functions, the Euler operator, the
\Gamma
operator, the Laplace operator, etc., or may take the form of an equivalence class of distributions, as is the case for e.g. division by analytic functions, the operators
\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r}
and
\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}}
, the operators
\underline{D}
and
d_{j},j=1,\ldots,m
, etc. The so–called spherical operators map distributions to signumdsitributions, and their action may be either uniquely determined, as is the case for the spherical derivatives
\partial_{r}
and
\partial_{\underline{\omega}}
, or may result into an equivalence class of signumdistributions, as is the case for division by the spherical distance
r
. Due to their homogeneity and their (signum–)radial character, the distributions
T_{\lambda}
and
U_{\lambda}
behave specifically under the action of all those operators, in the sense that the results of these actions all are uniquely determined, except for a small number of cases, viz.
\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r}\,U_{-m+1}
,
\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}}\,U_{-m+1}
,
\underline{D}\,U_{-m+1}
,
d_{j}\,U_{-m+1},j=1,\ldots,m
,
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,T_{-m+2}
and
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,U_{-m+1}
, which all can be traced back to the non–uniquely determined action
\frac{1}{\underline{x}}\,U_{-m+1}
. Nevertheless we were able to uniquely determine the forementioned actions conserving overall consistency. Crucial to obtaining this result is the possibility to have a free choice of the arbitrary constant in the equivalence class
\left[\frac{1}{\underline{x}}\,U_{-m+1}\right]
, the non–uniquely determined character of which should be maintained.

14
Appendix 1: The distribution
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
on the real line

Let
\mu
be a complex parameter and
x
a real variable. We consider the function

x_{+}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}\,Y(x)

where
Y(x)
is the Heaviside step function.

If
\Re\,\mu>-1
then
x_{+}^{\mu}
is locally integrable and hence a regular distribution given, for all test functions
\phi(x)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
, by

\langle\ x_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \phi(x)\ \rangle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,x^{\mu}\,\phi(x)\,dx\quad,\quad\Re\,\mu>-1

As a function of
\mu
the distribution
x_{+}^{\mu}
is holomorphic in the halfplane
\Re\,\mu>-1
.

For
\Re\,\mu\leq-1
the function
x_{+}^{\mu}
is no longer locally integrable. One introduces the so–calles
finite part
distribution
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
by putting for
-n-1<\Re\,\mu<-n
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\phi(x)\ \rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,x^{\mu}\,\left(\phi(x)-\phi(0)-\frac{\phi^{\prime}(0)}{1!}\,x-\cdots-\frac{\phi^{(n-1)}(0)}{(n-1)!}\,x^{n-1}\right)\,dx{}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\left(\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,x^{\mu}\,\phi(x)\,dx+\phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+1}}{\mu+1}+\cdots+\frac{\phi^{(n-1)}(0)}{(n-1)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+n}}{\mu+n}\right)

(122)

Note that for
\mu>-1
the defining expression for
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
(
122
) reduces to that for
x_{+}^{\mu}
, and by analytic continuation
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
becomes holomorphic in
\mathbb{C}\backslash\{-1,-2,-3,\ldots\}
. Its singularities
\mu=-1,-2,-3,\ldots
are simple poles with residue

{\rm Res}_{\mu=-n}\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,\delta^{(n-1)}(x)

It is still possible to define
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
for
\mu=-1,-2,-3,\ldots
through the so–called
monomial pseudofunctions
, but it should be emphasized that by this additional defintion no entire function of
\mu
is obtained. One puts, for all
n\in\mathbb{C}
,

\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-n}\ ,\phi(x)\ \rangle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\left(\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,x^{-n}\,\phi(x)\,dx+\phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{-n+1}}{-n+1}+\cdots+\frac{\phi^{(n-2)}(0)}{(n-2)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{-1}+\frac{\phi^{(n-1)}(0)}{(n-1)!}\,\ln\epsilon\right)

Lemma 4
.

The distribution
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
obeys the multiplication rules

•

x\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}={\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu+1}\quad,\quad\mu\neq-1,-2,-3,\dots

•

x\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-1}=Y(x)

•

x\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-n}={\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-n+1}\quad,\quad n=2,3,\ldots

Lemma 5
.

The distribution
{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}
obeys the differentiation rules

•

\partial_{x}\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}=\mu\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu-1}\quad,\quad\mu\neq 0,-1,-2,\dots

•

\partial_{x}\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{0}=\delta(x)

•

\partial_{x}\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-n}=(-n)\,{\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{-n-1}+(-1)^{n}\,\frac{1}{n!}\,\delta^{(n)}(x)\quad,\quad n=1,2,3,\ldots

Lemma 6
.

If the test function
\phi(x)
is such that
\phi(0)=\phi^{\prime}(0)=\ldots=\phi^{(k)}(0)=0
then

•

\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \frac{1}{x^{k}}\,\phi(x)\ \rangle=\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu-k}\ ,\ \phi(x)\ \rangle\quad,\quad\mu\neq 0,1,2,\ldots,k-1

•

\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{n}\ ,\ \frac{1}{x^{k}}\,\phi(x)\ \rangle=\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,x_{+}^{\mu-k}\ ,\ \phi(x)\ \rangle\quad,\quad n=0,1,2,\ldots,k-1

15
Appendix 2: The distributions
T_{\lambda}
and
U_{\lambda}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}

Let
\lambda
be a complex parameter, let
\mu=\lambda+m-1
, let Fp
r_{+}^{\mu}
be the finite part distribution on the real
r
–axis, defined by the monomial pseudofunctions for
\mu=-\,n,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\varphi(\underline{x})
be a scalar test function in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then we define the following two families of distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Definition 16
.

One puts, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,

\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}\,[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

and

\langle\ U_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}\,[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

The scalar distributions
T_{\lambda}
are classical distributions in harmonic analysis. The
1
–vector distributions
U_{\lambda}
are the Clifford analysis counterparts of vector valued distributions, see the history comment in
[
12
]
. Both families of distributions were thoroughly studied in the framework of Clifford analysis, see the references mentioned in the introductory Section
1
.

Seen the holomorphy properties of the one–dimensional distribution Fp
r_{+}^{\mu}
, we expect
T_{\lambda}
to be
a priori
a meromorphic function of the complex paramter
\lambda
with simple poles at
\mu=-1,-2,\ldots
, or
\lambda=-m,-m-1,-m-2,\ldots
with residues given by

\displaystyle{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell+1}\,\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ -\,\frac{1}{(2\ell-1)!}\,\delta^{(2\ell-1)}(r)\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}\,[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell}\,\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ \frac{1}{(2\ell)!}\,\delta^{(2\ell)}(r)\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}\,[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

As the spherical mean
\Sigma^{(0)}\,[\varphi](r)
has vanishing odd order derivatives at the origin
r=0
, it follows that the singularities of
T_{\lambda}
at the points
\lambda=-m-2\ell+1,\ell=1,2,\ldots
are removable. This should be confirmed by coinciding expressions for
T_{\lambda}
in the vertical strips
-m-2\ell-1<\Re\,\lambda<-m-2\ell
and
-m-2\ell-2<\Re\,\lambda<-m-2\ell-1
, and a matching value at the removable singularity
\lambda=-m-2\ell-1
. We indeed have, using the short–hand notation
\Phi
for the spherical mean
\Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi]
,

•

for
-m-2\ell-1<\Re\,\lambda<-m-2\ell
or
-2\ell-2<\Re\,\mu<-2\ell-1

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{\mu}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+1}}{\mu+1}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)}{1!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2}}{\mu+2}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell-1)}(0)}{(2\ell-1)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell}}{\mu+2\ell}+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell+1}}{\mu+2\ell+1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{\mu}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+1}}{\mu+1}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+3}}{\mu+3}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell+1}}{\mu+2\ell+1}

•

for
-m-2\ell-2<\Re\,\lambda<-m-2\ell-1
or
-2\ell-3<\Re\,\mu<-2\ell-2

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{\mu}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+1}}{\mu+1}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)}{1!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2}}{\mu+2}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell+1}}{\mu+2\ell+1}+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell+1)}(0)}{(2\ell+1)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell+2}}{\mu+2\ell+2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{\mu}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+1}}{\mu+1}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+3}}{\mu+3}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{\mu+2\ell+1}}{\mu+2\ell+1}

•

for
\lambda=-m-2\ell-1
or
\mu=-2\ell-2

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\langle\ T_{-m-2\ell-1}\ ,\ \varphi\ \rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{-2\ell-2}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{-2\ell-1}}{-2\ell-1}+\frac{\Phi^{\prime}(0)}{1!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{-2\ell}}{-2\ell}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{-1}+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell+1)}(0)}{(2\ell+1)!}\,\ln{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,r^{-2\ell-2}\,\Phi(r)\,dr+\Phi(0)\,\frac{\epsilon^{-2\ell-1}}{-2\ell-1}+\cdots+\frac{\Phi^{(2\ell)}(0)}{(2\ell)!}\,\frac{\epsilon^{-1}}{-1}

We may conclude that the distribution
T_{\lambda}
is a meromorphic function of the complex parameter
\lambda
showing simple poles at
\lambda=-m-2\ell,\ell=0,1,2,\ldots
where there is an
ad hoc
defintion through the monomial pseudofunctions. Let us compute the residue at these singularities:

\displaystyle{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell}\,\langle\ T_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle={\rm Res}_{\,\mu=-2\ell-1}\,a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\ell)!}\,a_{m}\,\langle\ \delta^{(2\ell)}(r)\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\,\frac{m+2\ell}{C(m,\ell)}\,a_{m}\,\{\underline{\partial}^{2\ell}\,\varphi(\underline{x})\}|_{\underline{x}=0}

whence

{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell}\,T_{\lambda}=(-1)^{\ell}\,\frac{m+2\ell}{C(m,\ell)}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2\ell}\,\delta(\underline{x})

In a similar way we eventually find that the distribution
U_{\lambda}
is a meromorphic function of the complex parameter
\lambda
showing simple poles at
\lambda=-m-2\ell-1,\ell=0,1,2,\ldots
where there is an
ad hoc
definition through the monomial pseudofunctions.
For the residue at these singularities we have

\displaystyle{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell-1}\,\langle\ U_{\lambda}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle={\rm Res}_{\,\mu=-2\ell-2}\,a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{\mu}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{1}{(2\ell+1)!}\,a_{m}\,\langle\ \delta^{(2\ell+1)}(r)\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\frac{1}{C(m,\ell)}\,a_{m}\,\{\underline{\partial}^{2\ell+1}\,\varphi(\underline{x})\}|_{\underline{x}=0}

whence

{\rm Res}_{\,\lambda=-m-2\ell-1}\,U_{\lambda}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\frac{1}{C(m,\ell)}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2\ell+1}\,\delta(\underline{x})

16
Appendix 3: Some specific distributions

In this appendix we give an overview of the properties of some specific distributions of the
T_{\lambda}
and
U_{\lambda}
families.

\boxed{\ \ T_{0}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ T_{0}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{m-1}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,r^{m-1}\,dr\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})d\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\langle\ 1\,,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

T_{0}=1
is the regular distribution associated to the constant function
1=\underline{x}^{0}
. Its properties are trivial.

\boxed{\ \ U_{0}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ U_{0}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{m-1}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,r^{m-1}\,dr\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\underline{\omega}\,\varphi(\underline{x})d\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\langle\ \underline{\omega}\,,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

U_{0}=\underline{\omega}
is the regular distribution associated to the function
\underline{\omega}=\frac{\underline{x}}{|\underline{x}|}
, which is the higher dimensional counterpart to the
signum distribution
on the real line. Its properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,U_{0}=-\,T_{1}=-\,r

•

\underline{\partial}\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-1}

which splits up into

(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,U_{0}=0\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-1}

•

\Delta\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-2}

•

\mathbb{E}\,U_{0}=0

•

\Gamma\,U_{0}=(m-1)\,U_{0}
and
\partial_{\underline{\omega}}\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)

•

\Delta^{*}\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)\,\underline{\omega}
and
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\Delta^{*}\,U_{0}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-2}

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,U_{0}=0
and
\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,U_{0}=0

•

\underline{D}\,U_{0}=0

•

{\bf Z}\,U_{0}=0

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,U_{0}=0

\boxed{\ \ T_{-m+2}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ T_{-m+2}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,r\,dr\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m-2}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})d\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\langle\ \frac{1}{r^{m-2}}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

T_{-m+2}
is the regular distribution associated to the locally integrable function
\frac{1}{r^{m-2}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Its properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,T_{-m+2}=U_{-m+3}

•

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-2)\,U_{-m+1}

which splits up into

(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-2)\,U_{-m+1}\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,T_{-m+2}=0

•

\Delta\,T_{-m+2}=-\,a_{m}\,(m-2)\,\delta(\underline{x})

which expresses the fact that
-\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\frac{1}{m-2}\,\frac{1}{r^{m-2}}
is the fundamental solution of the Laplace operator

•

\mathbb{E}\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-2)\,T_{-m+2}

•

\Gamma\,T_{-m+2}=0

•

\Delta^{*}\,T_{-m+2}=0

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,T_{-m+2}=(m-2)(m-1)\,T_{-m}-a_{m}\,(m-2)\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-2)\,T_{-m}

•

\underline{D}\,T_{-m+2}=U_{-m+1}

•

{\bf Z}\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m}+a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,T_{-m+2}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m}+(m-1)\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

\boxed{\ \ T_{-m+1}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ T_{-m+1}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{0}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,dr\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})d\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\langle\ \frac{1}{r^{m-1}}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

T_{-m+1}
is the regular distribution associated to the locally integrable function
\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Its properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,T_{-m+1}=U_{-m+2}

•

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m}

which splits up into

(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,T_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m}\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,T_{-m+1}=0

•

\Delta\,T_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,T_{-m-1}

•

\mathbb{E}\,T_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m+1}

•

\Gamma\,T_{-m+1}=0

•

\Delta^{*}\,T_{-m+1}=0

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,T_{-m+1}=(m-1)(m)\,T_{-m-1}

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,T_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m-1}

•

\underline{D}\,T_{-m+1}=0

•

{\bf Z}\,T_{-m+1}=0

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,T_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m-1}

\boxed{\ \ U_{-m+1}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ U_{-m+1}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{0}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,dr\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}\,\varphi(\underline{x})d\underline{x}

\displaystyle=\langle\ \frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m-1}}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

U_{-m+1}
is the regular distribution associated to the locally integrable function
\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Its properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,T_{-m+2}

•

\underline{\partial}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

which splits up into

(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,U_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,T_{-m}-a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,U_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m}

and expresses the fact that
-\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}=-\,\frac{1}{a_{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m}}\,\underline{x}
is the fundamental solution of the Dirac operator
\underline{\partial}

•

\Delta\,U_{-m+1}=a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

•

\mathbb{E}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m+1}

•

\Gamma\,U_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,U_{-m+1}

•

\Delta^{*}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m+1}
and
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\Delta^{*}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m-1}

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,U_{-m+1}=(m-1)(m)\,U_{-m-1}+\frac{2m-1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,U_{-m+1}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

•

\underline{D}\,U_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,T_{-m}-a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

{\bf Z}\,U_{-m+1}=(m-1)\,U_{-m-1}+a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,U_{-m+1}=0

\boxed{\ \ T_{-m}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ T_{-m}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{-1}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle={\rm Fp}\,\int_{0}^{+\infty}\,\frac{1}{r}\,dr\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,\frac{1}{r}\,\Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)+\varphi(0)\,\ln{\epsilon}

Properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,T_{-m}=U_{-m+1}

•

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-m}=-\,m\,U_{-m-1}-\,a_{m}\,\frac{1}{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

which splits up into

(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,T_{-m}=-\,m\,U_{-m-1}-\,a_{m}\,\frac{1}{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,T_{-m}=0

•

\Delta\,T_{-m}=2\,m\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{m+2}{2m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\mathbb{E}\,T_{-m}=-\,m\,T_{-m}+a_{m}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\Gamma\,T_{-m}=0

•

\Delta^{*}\,T_{-m}=0

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,T_{-m}=m(m+1)\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{2m+1}{2m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,T_{-m}=-\,m\,T_{-m-2}-\frac{1}{2m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\,\delta(\underline{x})

•

\underline{D}\,T_{-m}=-\,U_{-m-1}-\frac{1}{m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}\delta(\underline{x})

•

{\bf Z}\,T_{-m}=(m+1)\,T_{-m-2}+\frac{m+2}{2m}\,a_{m}\,\underline{\partial}^{2}\delta(\underline{x})

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,T_{-m}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m-2}

\boxed{\ \ U_{-m}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ U_{-m}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{-1}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle=a_{m}\,\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,\frac{1}{r}\,\Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](r)+\Sigma^{(1)}[\varphi](0)\,\ln{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\int_{\epsilon}^{+\infty}\,\frac{1}{r}\,dr\,\int_{S^{m-1}}\,\underline{\omega}\,\varphi(r\,\underline{\omega})\,dS_{\underline{\omega}}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash B(0,\epsilon)}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})\,d\underline{x}

\displaystyle={\rm Pv}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})\,d\underline{x}=\langle\ {\rm Pv}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

whence

U_{-m}={\rm Pv}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\,

which is the higher dimensional analogue of the
principal value
distribution Pv
\frac{1}{t}
on the real line. It is, up to a constant, the convolution kernel
\mathcal{H}
of the Hilbert transform, given, for a suitable function or distribution
f
, by

H[f]=\mathcal{H}*f=-\frac{2}{a_{m+1}}\,{\rm Pv}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\,*f

Properties are

•

\underline{x}\,U_{-m}=-\,T_{-m+1}
or
(r\,\underline{\omega})\,{\rm Pv}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}=-\,\frac{1}{r^{m-1}}

•

\underline{\partial}\,U_{-m}=T_{-m-1}
which splits up into
(\underline{\omega}\,\partial_{r})\,U_{-m}=m\,T_{-m-1}\quad{\rm and}\quad(\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\underline{\omega}})\,U_{-m}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m-1}

•

\Delta\,U_{-m}=(m+1)\,U_{-m-2}

•

\mathbb{E}\,U_{-m}=-\,m\,U_{-m}

•

\Gamma\,U_{-m}=(m-1)\,U_{-m}

•

\Delta^{*}\,U_{-m}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m}\quad{\rm and}\quad\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,\Delta^{*}\,U_{-m}=-\,(m-1)\,U_{-m-2}

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,U_{-m}=m(m+1)\,U_{-m-2}

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,U_{-m}=-\,m\,U_{-m-2}

•

\underline{D}\,U_{-m}=m\,T_{-m-1}

•

{\bf Z}\,U_{-m}=2\,m\,\,U_{-m-2}

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,U_{-m}=0

\boxed{\ \ T_{-m-1}\ \ }

One has

\displaystyle\langle\ T_{-m-1}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

\displaystyle=a_{m}\,\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,r_{+}^{-2}\ ,\ \Sigma^{(0)}[\varphi](r)\ \rangle

\displaystyle={\rm Fp}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}\,\varphi(\underline{x})\,d\underline{x}=\langle\ {\rm Fp}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}\ ,\ \varphi(\underline{x})\ \rangle

The scalar distribution
T_{-m-1}={\rm Fp}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}
is, up to a constant, the convolution kernel for the so–called
square root of the negative Laplacian
, which, for an appropriate function or distribution
f
, is defined by

\left(-\,\Delta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,[f]=-\,\frac{2}{a_{m+1}}\,{\rm Fp}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}*f

Properties are:

•

\underline{x}\,T_{-m-1}=U_{-m}

•

\underline{\partial}\,T_{-m-1}=-\,(m+1)\,U_{-m-2}

•

\Delta\,T_{-m-1}=3\,(m+1)\,T_{-m-3}

•

\mathbb{E}\,T_{-m-1}=-\,(m+1)\,T_{-m-1}

•

\Gamma\,T_{-m-1}=0

•

\Delta^{*}\,T_{-m-1}=0

•

\partial_{r}^{2}\,T_{-m-1}=(m+1)(m+2)\,T_{-m-3}

•

\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{r}\,T_{-m-1}=-\,(m+1)\,T_{-m-3}

•

\underline{D}\,T_{-m-1}=-2\,U_{-m-2}

•

{\bf Z}\,T_{-m-1}=2\,(m+2)\,\,T_{-m-3}

•

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\,{\bf Z}^{*}\,T_{-m-1}=-\,(m-1)\,T_{-m-3}

Note that in
[
13
]
the so–called Hilbert–Dirac operator
HD
was introduced as the convolution operator with convolution kernel
\underline{\partial}\,\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}\,\underline{\partial}
. As it holds that
\underline{\partial}\,U_{-m}=T_{-m-1}
or

\underline{\partial}\,\left(-\frac{2}{a_{m+1}}\,{\rm Pv}\,\frac{\underline{\omega}}{r^{m}}\right)=-\,\frac{2}{a_{m+1}}\,{\rm Fp}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}

it becomes clear that the convolution kernel of the operator
\left(-\,\Delta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is precisely the convolution kernel
\underline{\partial}\,\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}\,\underline{\partial}
of the Hilbert–Dirac operator
HD
, whence

HD[f]=\underline{\partial}\,\mathcal{H}\,*f=\mathcal{H}\,\underline{\partial}\,*f=\left(-\,\frac{2}{a_{m+1}}\,{\rm Fp}\,\frac{1}{r^{m+1}}\right)\,*f=\left(-\,\Delta\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,[f]
